========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, um, thank you. Well, I guess I'm here because... I've been having these, um, these eating episodes, I guess you could call them. And it's not even just the eating itself that's the main problem, it's really what happens right after. After these eating episodes, I just feel so incredibly gross. Like, just physically and mentally disgusted with myself. I just sit there and I feel so deeply disappointed in myself, because I really feel like I should have more control, you know? I mean, I’m an adult, I work hard cleaning buildings all week, I should be able to just stop myself or have some basic willpower. But I don't. 

And then the guilt just spirals, and I end up pulling out my journal. I’ve kept a journal for years, mostly just to vent about work or when I was taking college classes, but now... now I just end up writing the most awful, terrible things about myself in it. Just pages and pages of me tearing myself apart and calling myself names because I feel so out of control. I just sit there looking at what I did and write about how weak I am. It's just gotten to be too much, carrying around all that disappointment and hate for myself every time this happens.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like, once I start, I just can't stop. It's almost automatic, you know? Like my hand just keeps reaching for the next thing, and the next thing, and it doesn't even feel like a conscious choice after a certain point. I'll be sitting there, and in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish, you have to be up early for work, just put the bag down.' I love drawing, I do these little charcoal sketches, and I'll have my sketchbook right there on the table, and I'll be telling myself to just pick up the pencil instead of the food. 

But it's like my brain and my body are completely disconnected. The rational part of me is screaming to stop, but I just keep going. I don't actually stop until all the food is completely gone, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't fit anything else in and I'm just sitting there in pain. It's so frustrating because I can hear myself telling me to stop, I'm so aware of it in the moment, but I just... I don't. It's like I'm on autopilot, just totally out of control, and no matter how much I try to reason with myself while it's happening, the food just wins every time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some people do, I've read about it, but I just... I don't. I don't throw up. The thought of throwing up actually really scares me, to be honest. When I was younger, I got food poisoning once from this little diner near my apartment—it was awful, I was sick for three days straight, couldn't even keep water down—and ever since then, I just can't stand the feeling of nausea or vomiting. So, no, I definitely don't do that. 

And as for exercising... well, I'm on my feet all day for work. Cleaning offices and scrubbing floors, you're constantly moving, bending, lifting heavy trash bags, pushing those big industrial carts around. By the time I get home, my legs feel like lead, and my lower back is just aching. The last thing I want to do is go for a run or hit a gym. I mean, I used to try doing those little home workout videos back when I was taking college classes, just to relieve stress, but now I just don't have the energy. 

So, yeah, after an episode, I just kind of sit there. I don't try to burn it off or get rid of it. I just sit there with my journal, feeling miserable and full, and let the guilt wash over me. I just take it, I guess. I don't try to undo it, because I know I did it to myself, so I just sit with the physical discomfort and the mental disgust until I eventually drag myself to bed.

It's like I'm on autopilot, just totally out of control, and no matter how much I try to reason with myself while it's happening, the food just wins every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some people do, I've read about it, but I just... I don't. I don't throw up. The thought of throwing up actually really scares me, to be honest. When I was younger, I got food poisoning once from this little diner near my apartment—it was awful, I was sick for three days straight, couldn't even keep water down—and ever since then, I just can't stand the feeling of nausea or vomiting. So, no, I definitely don't do that. \n\nAnd as for exercising... well, I'm on my feet all day for work. Cleaning offices and scrubbing floors, you're constantly moving, bending, lifting heavy trash bags, pushing those big industrial carts around. I mean, I used to try doing those little home workout videos back when I was taking college classes, just to relieve stress, but now I just don't have the energy. \n\nSo, yeah, after an episode, I just kind of sit there. I don't try to burn it off or get rid of it. I just sit there with my journal, feeling miserable and full, and let the guilt wash over me. I just take it, I guess. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I mean, for the past several months, it feels like it's just become this awful routine. Usually, there's at least one night each week where it happens. It's almost always after work. I'll finish my shift, and I'm just so tired from being on my feet, sweeping and mopping and dealing with the industrial cleaners all day, and I'll stop by the grocery store on my way home. I always tell myself I'm just going in to get a normal dinner, maybe some chicken or a salad, but then I just start grabbing things. Cookies, chips, a whole pizza, ice cream—just way too much food. 

And then I get back to my apartment, and I just eat it all in one stretch. Just sit there on my couch, maybe put on a show I don't even really watch, and I just consume everything I bought until it's gone. And honestly, I can't even remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Like, maybe there was one week where I managed to just come home and eat a normal meal and go to sleep, but then the very next week, I'm right back at the store, filling up a basket with junk food. It's just this constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I tell myself that *this* week is going to be different, that *this* week I'm going to be better. But it just keeps happening, week after week.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' 

But then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. 

I'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.

I love drawing, I do these little charcoal sketches, and I'll have my sketchbook right there on the table, and I'll be telling myself to just pick up the pencil instead of the food. \n\nBut it's like my brain and my body are completely disconnected. The rational part of me is screaming to stop, but I just keep going. I don't actually stop until all the food is completely gone, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't fit anything else in and I'm just sitting there in pain. It's so frustrating because I can hear myself telling me to stop, I'm so aware of it in the moment, but I just... I don't. It's like I'm on autopilot, just totally out of control, and no matter how much I try to reason with myself while it's happening, the food just wins every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some people do, I've read about it, but I just... I don't. I don't throw up. The thought of throwing up actually really scares me, to be honest. When I was younger, I got food poisoning once from this little diner near my apartment—it was awful, I was sick for three days straight, couldn't even keep water down—and ever since then, I just can't stand the feeling of nausea or vomiting. So, no, I definitely don't do that. \n\nAnd as for exercising... well, I'm on my feet all day for work. Cleaning offices and scrubbing floors, you're constantly moving, bending, lifting heavy trash bags, pushing those big industrial carts around. By the time I get home, my legs feel like lead, and my lower back is just aching. The last thing I want to do is go for a run or hit a gym. I mean, I used to try doing those little home workout videos back when I was taking college classes, just to relieve stress, but now I just don't have the energy. \n\nSo, yeah, after an episode, I just kind of sit there. I don't try to burn it off or get rid of it. I just sit there with my journal, feeling miserable and full, and let the guilt wash over me. I just take it, I guess. I don't try to undo it, because I know I did it to myself, so I just sit with the physical discomfort and the mental disgust until I eventually drag myself to bed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I mean, for the past several months, it feels like it's just become this awful routine. Usually, there's at least one night each week where it happens. It's almost always after work. I'll finish my shift, and I'm just so tired from being on my feet, sweeping and mopping and dealing with the industrial cleaners all day, and I'll stop by the grocery store on my way home. I always tell myself I'm just going in to get a normal dinner, maybe some chicken or a salad, but then I just start grabbing things. Cookies, chips, a whole pizza, ice cream—just way too much food. \n\nAnd then I get back to my apartment, and I just eat it all in one stretch. Just sit there on my couch, maybe put on a show I don't even really watch, and I just consume everything I bought until it's gone. And honestly, I can't even remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Like, maybe there was one week where I managed to just come home and eat a normal meal and go to sleep, but then the very next week, I'm right back at the store, filling up a basket with junk food. It's just this constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I tell myself that *this* week is going to be different, that *this* week I'm going to be better. But it just keeps happening, week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.

But... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. So, yeah, I guess it doesn't stop me from functioning, like, I still pay my rent and show up where I need to be, but it definitely casts this huge, dark shadow over everything else I try to do.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, um, thank you. Well, I guess I'm here because... I've been having these, um, these eating episodes, I guess you could call them. And it's not even just the eating itself that's the main problem, it's really what happens right after. After these eating episodes, I just feel so incredibly gross. Like, just physically and mentally disgusted with myself. I just sit there and I feel so deeply disappointed in myself, because I really feel like I should have more control, you know? I mean, I’m an adult, I work hard cleaning buildings all week, I should be able to just stop myself or have some basic willpower. But I don't. \n\nAnd then the guilt just spirals, and I end up pulling out my journal. I’ve kept a journal for years, mostly just to vent about work or when I was taking college classes, but now... now I just end up writing the most awful, terrible things about myself in it. Just pages and pages of me tearing myself apart and calling myself names because I feel so out of control. I just sit there looking at what I did and write about how weak I am. It's just gotten to be too much, carrying around all that disappointment and hate for myself every time this happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. Like my hand just keeps reaching for the next thing, and the next thing, and it doesn't even feel like a conscious choice after a certain point. I don't try to undo it, because I know I did it to myself, so I just sit with the physical discomfort and the mental disgust until I eventually drag myself to bed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now. Cookies, chips, a whole pizza, ice cream—just way too much food. \n\nAnd then I get back to my apartment, and I just eat it all in one stretch. Just sit there on my couch, maybe put on a show I don't even really watch, and I just consume everything I bought until it's gone. And honestly, I can't even remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Like, maybe there was one week where I managed to just come home and eat a normal meal and go to sleep, but then the very next week, I'm right back at the store, filling up a basket with junk food. It's just this constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I tell myself that *this* week is going to be different, that *this* week I'm going to be better. But it just keeps happening, week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes and the feelings that follow significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.\n\nBut... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. So, yeah, I guess it doesn't stop me from functioning, like, I still pay my rent and show up where I need to be, but it definitely casts this huge, dark shadow over everything else I try to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I can’t remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was kind of mentioning earlier, it's usually just the one time. I mean, for the past several months, it's been pretty consistently at least one night each week where I do that whole routine—you know, stopping after work, buying way too much food, and then just sitting there on my couch and eating it all in one stretch. 

Occasionally it might happen a second time if it's been a really, really bad week at the building—like if someone spilled something awful in the lobby or if I'm just feeling extra down about not being in my design classes—but generally, it's just that one night a week. And I've actually tried to think back, like, looking at the calendar app on my phone to see if there was a time I managed to break the cycle, but honestly, I can't remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. 

It's like I can never quite make it to that two-week mark. I'll get through five or six days and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, Fumi, you're doing so well, you're just eating normal dinners,' and then bam, I'm right back at the store with my arms full of ramen and sweets. It's just so frustrating because it's like clockwork. You'd think if it's mostly just once a week, I could just power through that one evening and stop myself, but I just can't seem to do it. It just keeps happening, week after week after week.

Like, just physically and mentally disgusted with myself. I just sit there and I feel so deeply disappointed in myself, because I really feel like I should have more control, you know? I mean, I’m an adult, I work hard cleaning buildings all week, I should be able to just stop myself or have some basic willpower. But I don't. \n\nAnd then the guilt just spirals, and I end up pulling out my journal. I’ve kept a journal for years, mostly just to vent about work or when I was taking college classes, but now... now I just end up writing the most awful, terrible things about myself in it. Just pages and pages of me tearing myself apart and calling myself names because I feel so out of control. I just sit there looking at what I did and write about how weak I am. It's just gotten to be too much, carrying around all that disappointment and hate for myself every time this happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like, once I start, I just can't stop. It's almost automatic, you know? Like my hand just keeps reaching for the next thing, and the next thing, and it doesn't even feel like a conscious choice after a certain point. I'll be sitting there, and in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish, you have to be up early for work, just put the bag down.' I love drawing, I do these little charcoal sketches, and I'll have my sketchbook right there on the table, and I'll be telling myself to just pick up the pencil instead of the food. \n\nBut it's like my brain and my body are completely disconnected. The rational part of me is screaming to stop, but I just keep going. I don't actually stop until all the food is completely gone, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't fit anything else in and I'm just sitting there in pain. It's so frustrating because I can hear myself telling me to stop, I'm so aware of it in the moment, but I just... I don't. It's like I'm on autopilot, just totally out of control, and no matter how much I try to reason with myself while it's happening, the food just wins every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some people do, I've read about it, but I just... I don't. I don't throw up. The thought of throwing up actually really scares me, to be honest. When I was younger, I got food poisoning once from this little diner near my apartment—it was awful, I was sick for three days straight, couldn't even keep water down—and ever since then, I just can't stand the feeling of nausea or vomiting. So, no, I definitely don't do that. \n\nAnd as for exercising... well, I'm on my feet all day for work. Cleaning offices and scrubbing floors, you're constantly moving, bending, lifting heavy trash bags, pushing those big industrial carts around. By the time I get home, my legs feel like lead, and my lower back is just aching. The last thing I want to do is go for a run or hit a gym. I mean, I used to try doing those little home workout videos back when I was taking college classes, just to relieve stress, but now I just don't have the energy. \n\nSo, yeah, after an episode, I just kind of sit there. I don't try to burn it off or get rid of it. I just sit there with my journal, feeling miserable and full, and let the guilt wash over me. I just take it, I guess. I don't try to undo it, because I know I did it to myself, so I just sit with the physical discomfort and the mental disgust until I eventually drag myself to bed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I mean, for the past several months, it feels like it's just become this awful routine. Usually, there's at least one night each week where it happens. It's almost always after work. I'll finish my shift, and I'm just so tired from being on my feet, sweeping and mopping and dealing with the industrial cleaners all day, and I'll stop by the grocery store on my way home. I always tell myself I'm just going in to get a normal dinner, maybe some chicken or a salad, but then I just start grabbing things. Cookies, chips, a whole pizza, ice cream—just way too much food. \n\nAnd then I get back to my apartment, and I just eat it all in one stretch. Just sit there on my couch, maybe put on a show I don't even really watch, and I just consume everything I bought until it's gone. And honestly, I can't even remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Like, maybe there was one week where I managed to just come home and eat a normal meal and go to sleep, but then the very next week, I'm right back at the store, filling up a basket with junk food. It's just this constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I tell myself that *this* week is going to be different, that *this* week I'm going to be better. But it just keeps happening, week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes and the feelings that follow significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.\n\nBut... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. I can’t remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still make it to my cleaning shifts and design events, but the next day I feel distracted and ashamed, and sometimes I put off sketching because I don’t want to look at the journal entries I wrote afterward.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 2 months, since I started trying to switch from cleaning work into design while keeping my old shifts.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 24 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S055\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes and the feelings that follow significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.\n\nBut... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. So, yeah, I guess it doesn't stop me from functioning, like, I still pay my rent and show up where I need to be, but it definitely casts this huge, dark shadow over everything else I try to do.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes happen more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was kind of mentioning earlier, it's usually just the one time. I mean, for the past several months, it's been pretty consistently at least one night each week where I do that whole routine—you know, stopping after work, buying way too much food, and then just sitting there on my couch and eating it all in one stretch. \n\nOccasionally it might happen a second time if it's been a really, really bad week at the building—like if someone spilled something awful in the lobby or if I'm just feeling extra down about not being in my design classes—but generally, it's just that one night a week. And I've actually tried to think back, like, looking at the calendar app on my phone to see if there was a time I managed to break the cycle, but honestly, I can't remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. \n\nIt's like I can never quite make it to that two-week mark. I'll get through five or six days and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, Fumi, you're doing so well, you're just eating normal dinners,' and then bam, I'm right back at the store with my arms full of ramen and sweets. It's just so frustrating because it's like clockwork. You'd think if it's mostly just once a week, I could just power through that one evening and stop myself, but I just can't seem to do it. It just keeps happening, week after week after week.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating or the pace at which you're eating during these episodes?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like I completely lose the steering wheel, you know? Like, I'll be sitting there on my bed, still in my uniform, and I've just finished the ramen, and before I even really register what I'm doing, my hand is already unwrapping one of those tuna mayo rice balls. And it's not like I'm hungry anymore, not really, but I just keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. 

It's this really weird disconnect. Because in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop it, you don't need this, you have sketches to finish, you wanted to practice that shading technique tonight.' But my body just isn't listening to my brain. I just keep chewing and swallowing and reaching for the fried chicken, and then the gummy candies. I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, every last wrapper empty, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't force another bite down. It's like a runaway train, and I'm just watching it crash from the inside. I hate it so much because I know I should be able to just put the food down, but in that moment, I just... I can't. I just can't stop.

Like, just physically and mentally disgusted with myself. I just sit there and I feel so deeply disappointed in myself, because I really feel like I should have more control, you know? Just pages and pages of me tearing myself apart and calling myself names because I feel so out of control. I just sit there looking at what I did and write about how weak I am. It's just gotten to be too much, carrying around all that disappointment and hate for myself every time this happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. I mean, I know some people do, I've read about it, but I just... I don't. I don't throw up. The thought of throwing up actually really scares me, to be honest. When I was younger, I got food poisoning once from this little diner near my apartment—it was awful, I was sick for three days straight, couldn't even keep water down—and ever since then, I just can't stand the feeling of nausea or vomiting. I mean, I’m an adult, I work hard cleaning buildings all week, I should be able to just stop myself or have some basic willpower. But I don't. \n\nAnd then the guilt just spirals, and I end up pulling out my journal. I’ve kept a journal for years, mostly just to vent about work or when I was taking college classes, but now... now I just end up writing the most awful, terrible things about myself in it. Just pages and pages of me tearing myself apart and calling myself names because I feel so out of control. I just sit there looking at what I did and write about how weak I am. It's just gotten to be too much, carrying around all that disappointment and hate for myself every time this happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like, once I start, I just can't stop. It's almost automatic, you know? Like my hand just keeps reaching for the next thing, and the next thing, and it doesn't even feel like a conscious choice after a certain point. I'll be sitting there, and in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish, you have to be up early for work, just put the bag down.' I love drawing, I do these little charcoal sketches, and I'll have my sketchbook right there on the table, and I'll be telling myself to just pick up the pencil instead of the food. \n\nBut it's like my brain and my body are completely disconnected. The rational part of me is screaming to stop, but I just keep going. I don't actually stop until all the food is completely gone, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't fit anything else in and I'm just sitting there in pain. It's so frustrating because I can hear myself telling me to stop, I'm so aware of it in the moment, but I just... I don't. It's like I'm on autopilot, just totally out of control, and no matter how much I try to reason with myself while it's happening, the food just wins every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some people do, I've read about it, but I just... I don't. I don't throw up. The thought of throwing up actually really scares me, to be honest. When I was younger, I got food poisoning once from this little diner near my apartment—it was awful, I was sick for three days straight, couldn't even keep water down—and ever since then, I just can't stand the feeling of nausea or vomiting. So, no, I definitely don't do that. \n\nAnd as for exercising... well, I'm on my feet all day for work. Cleaning offices and scrubbing floors, you're constantly moving, bending, lifting heavy trash bags, pushing those big industrial carts around. By the time I get home, my legs feel like lead, and my lower back is just aching. The last thing I want to do is go for a run or hit a gym. I mean, I used to try doing those little home workout videos back when I was taking college classes, just to relieve stress, but now I just don't have the energy. \n\nSo, yeah, after an episode, I just kind of sit there. I don't try to burn it off or get rid of it. I just sit there with my journal, feeling miserable and full, and let the guilt wash over me. I just take it, I guess. I don't try to undo it, because I know I did it to myself, so I just sit with the physical discomfort and the mental disgust until I eventually drag myself to bed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I mean, for the past several months, it feels like it's just become this awful routine. Usually, there's at least one night each week where it happens. It's almost always after work. I'll finish my shift, and I'm just so tired from being on my feet, sweeping and mopping and dealing with the industrial cleaners all day, and I'll stop by the grocery store on my way home. I always tell myself I'm just going in to get a normal dinner, maybe some chicken or a salad, but then I just start grabbing things. Cookies, chips, a whole pizza, ice cream—just way too much food. \n\nAnd then I get back to my apartment, and I just eat it all in one stretch. Just sit there on my couch, maybe put on a show I don't even really watch, and I just consume everything I bought until it's gone. And honestly, I can't even remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Like, maybe there was one week where I managed to just come home and eat a normal meal and go to sleep, but then the very next week, I'm right back at the store, filling up a basket with junk food. It's just this constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I tell myself that *this* week is going to be different, that *this* week I'm going to be better. But it just keeps happening, week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes and the feelings that follow significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.\n\nBut... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. So, yeah, I guess it doesn't stop me from functioning, like, I still pay my rent and show up where I need to be, but it definitely casts this huge, dark shadow over everything else I try to do.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes happen more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was kind of mentioning earlier, it's usually just the one time. I mean, for the past several months, it's been pretty consistently at least one night each week where I do that whole routine—you know, stopping after work, buying way too much food, and then just sitting there on my couch and eating it all in one stretch. \n\nOccasionally it might happen a second time if it's been a really, really bad week at the building—like if someone spilled something awful in the lobby or if I'm just feeling extra down about not being in my design classes—but generally, it's just that one night a week. And I've actually tried to think back, like, looking at the calendar app on my phone to see if there was a time I managed to break the cycle, but honestly, I can't remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. \n\nIt's like I can never quite make it to that two-week mark. I'll get through five or six days and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, Fumi, you're doing so well, you're just eating normal dinners,' and then bam, I'm right back at the store with my arms full of ramen and sweets. It's just so frustrating because it's like clockwork. You'd think if it's mostly just once a week, I could just power through that one evening and stop myself, but I just can't seem to do it. It just keeps happening, week after week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating or the pace at which you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like I completely lose the steering wheel, you know? Like, I'll be sitting there on my bed, still in my uniform, and I've just finished the ramen, and before I even really register what I'm doing, my hand is already unwrapping one of those tuna mayo rice balls. And it's not like I'm hungry anymore, not really, but I just keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. \n\nIt's this really weird disconnect. Because in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop it, you don't need this, you have sketches to finish, you wanted to practice that shading technique tonight.' But my body just isn't listening to my brain. I just keep chewing and swallowing and reaching for the fried chicken, and then the gummy candies. I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, every last wrapper empty, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't force another bite down. It's like a runaway train, and I'm just watching it crash from the inside. I hate it so much because I know I should be able to just put the food down, but in that moment, I just... I can't. I just can't stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, um... not really, no. I mean, it's funny you ask that because you'd think, with all the junk I'm eating on those nights, that I'd be putting on a ton of weight, right? But honestly, my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did when I got hired six months ago. They're these terrible, stiff navy blue trousers, and they have this really unforgiving waistband, so I feel like I would definitely notice if I was gaining weight because they'd start digging into my stomach when I'm bending over to mop or whatever. 

But no, my weight has been pretty stable. I don't own a scale—I used to, back when I was still in school and living in the dorms, but when I moved into my current apartment after I dropped out, I just never bothered to buy a new one. But I can just tell by how my clothes fit. Like, even my favorite pair of jeans, the ones I wear when I go to those design events, they still zip up just fine. 

I guess maybe it's because my job is so physical? I'm on my feet for eight hours a day, walking up and down hallways, pushing that heavy cart, scrubbing things. It's a lot of manual labor, so maybe I'm just burning off all those extra calories from the ramen and the fried chicken? Or maybe because I don't eat much the day after a binge, because I feel so sick and guilty, it kind of balances out? I don't know. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. It's just the same as it's been for a while now.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, um, thank you. Well, I guess I'm here because... I've been having these, um, these eating episodes, I guess you could call them. And it's not even just the eating itself that's the main problem, it's really what happens right after. After these eating episodes, I just feel so incredibly gross. Like, just physically and mentally disgusted with myself. I just sit there and I feel so deeply disappointed in myself, because I really feel like I should have more control, you know? I mean, I’m an adult, I work hard cleaning buildings all week, I should be able to just stop myself or have some basic willpower. But I don't. \n\nAnd then the guilt just spirals, and I end up pulling out my journal. I’ve kept a journal for years, mostly just to vent about work or when I was taking college classes, but now... now I just end up writing the most awful, terrible things about myself in it. Just pages and pages of me tearing myself apart and calling myself names because I feel so out of control. I just sit there looking at what I did and write about how weak I am. It's just gotten to be too much, carrying around all that disappointment and hate for myself every time this happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like, once I start, I just can't stop. It's almost automatic, you know? Like my hand just keeps reaching for the next thing, and the next thing, and it doesn't even feel like a conscious choice after a certain point. I'll be sitting there, and in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish, you have to be up early for work, just put the bag down.' I love drawing, I do these little charcoal sketches, and I'll have my sketchbook right there on the table, and I'll be telling myself to just pick up the pencil instead of the food. \n\nBut it's like my brain and my body are completely disconnected. The rational part of me is screaming to stop, but I just keep going. I don't actually stop until all the food is completely gone, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't fit anything else in and I'm just sitting there in pain. It's so frustrating because I can hear myself telling me to stop, I'm so aware of it in the moment, but I just... I don't. It's like I'm on autopilot, just totally out of control, and no matter how much I try to reason with myself while it's happening, the food just wins every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some people do, I've read about it, but I just... I don't. I don't throw up. The thought of throwing up actually really scares me, to be honest. When I was younger, I got food poisoning once from this little diner near my apartment—it was awful, I was sick for three days straight, couldn't even keep water down—and ever since then, I just can't stand the feeling of nausea or vomiting. So, no, I definitely don't do that. \n\nAnd as for exercising... well, I'm on my feet all day for work. Cleaning offices and scrubbing floors, you're constantly moving, bending, lifting heavy trash bags, pushing those big industrial carts around. By the time I get home, my legs feel like lead, and my lower back is just aching. The last thing I want to do is go for a run or hit a gym. I mean, I used to try doing those little home workout videos back when I was taking college classes, just to relieve stress, but now I just don't have the energy. \n\nSo, yeah, after an episode, I just kind of sit there. I don't try to burn it off or get rid of it. I just sit there with my journal, feeling miserable and full, and let the guilt wash over me. I just take it, I guess. I don't try to undo it, because I know I did it to myself, so I just sit with the physical discomfort and the mental disgust until I eventually drag myself to bed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I mean, for the past several months, it feels like it's just become this awful routine. Usually, there's at least one night each week where it happens. It's almost always after work. I'll finish my shift, and I'm just so tired from being on my feet, sweeping and mopping and dealing with the industrial cleaners all day, and I'll stop by the grocery store on my way home. I always tell myself I'm just going in to get a normal dinner, maybe some chicken or a salad, but then I just start grabbing things. Cookies, chips, a whole pizza, ice cream—just way too much food. \n\nAnd then I get back to my apartment, and I just eat it all in one stretch. Just sit there on my couch, maybe put on a show I don't even really watch, and I just consume everything I bought until it's gone. And honestly, I can't even remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Like, maybe there was one week where I managed to just come home and eat a normal meal and go to sleep, but then the very next week, I'm right back at the store, filling up a basket with junk food. It's just this constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I tell myself that *this* week is going to be different, that *this* week I'm going to be better. But it just keeps happening, week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes and the feelings that follow significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.\n\nBut... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. So, yeah, I guess it doesn't stop me from functioning, like, I still pay my rent and show up where I need to be, but it definitely casts this huge, dark shadow over everything else I try to do.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes happen more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was kind of mentioning earlier, it's usually just the one time. I mean, for the past several months, it's been pretty consistently at least one night each week where I do that whole routine—you know, stopping after work, buying way too much food, and then just sitting there on my couch and eating it all in one stretch. \n\nOccasionally it might happen a second time if it's been a really, really bad week at the building—like if someone spilled something awful in the lobby or if I'm just feeling extra down about not being in my design classes—but generally, it's just that one night a week. And I've actually tried to think back, like, looking at the calendar app on my phone to see if there was a time I managed to break the cycle, but honestly, I can't remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. \n\nIt's like I can never quite make it to that two-week mark. I'll get through five or six days and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, Fumi, you're doing so well, you're just eating normal dinners,' and then bam, I'm right back at the store with my arms full of ramen and sweets. It's just so frustrating because it's like clockwork. You'd think if it's mostly just once a week, I could just power through that one evening and stop myself, but I just can't seem to do it. It just keeps happening, week after week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating or the pace at which you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like I completely lose the steering wheel, you know? Like, I'll be sitting there on my bed, still in my uniform, and I've just finished the ramen, and before I even really register what I'm doing, my hand is already unwrapping one of those tuna mayo rice balls. And it's not like I'm hungry anymore, not really, but I just keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. \n\nIt's this really weird disconnect. Because in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop it, you don't need this, you have sketches to finish, you wanted to practice that shading technique tonight.' But my body just isn't listening to my brain. I just keep chewing and swallowing and reaching for the fried chicken, and then the gummy candies. I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, every last wrapper empty, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't force another bite down. It's like a runaway train, and I'm just watching it crash from the inside. I hate it so much because I know I should be able to just put the food down, but in that moment, I just... I can't. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, it's funny you ask that because you'd think, with all the junk I'm eating on those nights, that I'd be putting on a ton of weight, right? But honestly, my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did when I got hired six months ago. They're these terrible, stiff navy blue trousers, and they have this really unforgiving waistband, so I feel like I would definitely notice if I was gaining weight because they'd start digging into my stomach when I'm bending over to mop or whatever. \n\nBut no, my weight has been pretty stable. I don't own a scale—I used to, back when I was still in school and living in the dorms, but when I moved into my current apartment after I dropped out, I just never bothered to buy a new one. But I can just tell by how my clothes fit. Like, even my favorite pair of jeans, the ones I wear when I go to those design events, they still zip up just fine. \n\nI guess maybe it's because my job is so physical? I'm on my feet for eight hours a day, walking up and down hallways, pushing that heavy cart, scrubbing things. It's a lot of manual labor, so maybe I'm just burning off all those extra calories from the ramen and the fried chicken? Or maybe because I don't eat much the day after a binge, because I feel so sick and guilty, it kind of balances out? I don't know. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. It's just the same as it's been for a while now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

And I've actually tried to think back, like, looking at the calendar app on my phone to see if there was a time I managed to break the cycle, but honestly, I can't remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. \n\nIt's like I can never quite make it to that two-week mark. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. Every single time. It's actually... well, it's the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because the physical part is bad, feeling so full and sick, but the mental part afterwards is just... it's exhausting. 

The minute I finish that last bite, or when I finally just physically can't eat anymore, this wave of just absolute disgust washes over me. I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. And it's not just feeling physically gross, it's this really intense disappointment in myself. I just sit there thinking, 'Fumi, you are twenty-four years old, you should be able to control yourself. You should have more willpower than a child in a candy store.' 

And then I usually end up grabbing my journal—the one I'm supposed to be using for my design ideas and sketching, you know, to keep my creative skills sharp—and instead of drawing, I just start writing these really awful, hateful things about myself. Pages and pages of it. I'll write about how weak I am, how I'm a failure, how I couldn't even finish college and now I can't even eat dinner like a normal person. I just tear myself apart. 

And I'll read back over it the next day and it just makes me feel even worse, but in the moment, I just can't stop the self-criticism. It's like I have to punish myself for what I just did. I'll sit up for hours just berating myself in my head and on paper, completely ruining whatever sleep I was supposed to get before my early shift. So, yeah... the guilt is always there, every single time, and it's honestly the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely drains whatever little bit of self-esteem I have left after a long week of scrubbing floors.

It's like I'm on autopilot, just totally out of control, and no matter how much I try to reason with myself while it's happening, the food just wins every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some people do, I've read about it, but I just... I don't. I don't throw up. The thought of throwing up actually really scares me, to be honest. When I was younger, I got food poisoning once from this little diner near my apartment—it was awful, I was sick for three days straight, couldn't even keep water down—and ever since then, I just can't stand the feeling of nausea or vomiting. So, no, I definitely don't do that. \n\nAnd as for exercising... well, I'm on my feet all day for work. I mean, I used to try doing those little home workout videos back when I was taking college classes, just to relieve stress, but now I just don't have the energy. \n\nSo, yeah, after an episode, I just kind of sit there. I don't try to burn it off or get rid of it. I just sit there with my journal, feeling miserable and full, and let the guilt wash over me. I just take it, I guess. I mean, for the past several months, it feels like it's just become this awful routine. Usually, there's at least one night each week where it happens. It's almost always after work. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. Because the physical part is bad, feeling so full and sick, but the mental part afterwards is just... it's exhausting. \n\nThe minute I finish that last bite, or when I finally just physically can't eat anymore, this wave of just absolute disgust washes over me. I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. You should have more willpower than a child in a candy store.' \n\nAnd then I usually end up grabbing my journal—the one I'm supposed to be using for my design ideas and sketching, you know, to keep my creative skills sharp—and instead of drawing, I just start writing these really awful, hateful things about myself. Pages and pages of it. I'll write about how weak I am, how I'm a failure, how I couldn't even finish college and now I can't even eat dinner like a normal person. I just tear myself apart. \n\nAnd I'll read back over it the next day and it just makes me feel even worse, but in the moment, I just can't stop the self-criticism. It's like I have to punish myself for what I just did. I'll sit up for hours just berating myself in my head and on paper, completely ruining whatever sleep I was supposed to get before my early shift.
