Because in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop it, you don't need this, you have sketches to finish, you wanted to practice that shading technique tonight.' But my body just isn't listening to my brain. I just keep chewing and swallowing and reaching for the fried chicken, and then the gummy candies. I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, every last wrapper empty, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't force another bite down. It's like a runaway train, and I'm just watching it crash from the inside. I hate it so much because I know I should be able to just put the food down, but in that moment, I just... I can't. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, it's funny you ask that because you'd think, with all the junk I'm eating on those nights, that I'd be putting on a ton of weight, right? But honestly, my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did when I got hired six months ago. They're these terrible, stiff navy blue trousers, and they have this really unforgiving waistband, so I feel like I would definitely notice if I was gaining weight because they'd start digging into my stomach when I'm bending over to mop or whatever. \n\nBut no, my weight has been pretty stable. I don't own a scale—I used to, back when I was still in school and living in the dorms, but when I moved into my current apartment after I dropped out, I just never bothered to buy a new one. But I can just tell by how my clothes fit. Like, even my favorite pair of jeans, the ones I wear when I go to those design events, they still zip up just fine. \n\nI guess maybe it's because my job is so physical? I'm on my feet for eight hours a day, walking up and down hallways, pushing that heavy cart, scrubbing things. It's a lot of manual labor, so maybe I'm just burning off all those extra calories from the ramen and the fried chicken? Or maybe because I don't eat much the day after a binge, because I feel so sick and guilty, it kind of balances out? I don't know. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. It's just the same as it's been for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself after these eating episodes every time they happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Every single time. It's actually... well, it's the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because the physical part is bad, feeling so full and sick, but the mental part afterwards is just... it's exhausting. \n\nThe minute I finish that last bite, or when I finally just physically can't eat anymore, this wave of just absolute disgust washes over me. I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. And it's not just feeling physically gross, it's this really intense disappointment in myself. I just sit there thinking, 'Fumi, you are twenty-four years old, you should be able to control yourself. You should have more willpower than a child in a candy store.' \n\nAnd then I usually end up grabbing my journal—the one I'm supposed to be using for my design ideas and sketching, you know, to keep my creative skills sharp—and instead of drawing, I just start writing these really awful, hateful things about myself. Pages and pages of it. I'll write about how weak I am, how I'm a failure, how I couldn't even finish college and now I can't even eat dinner like a normal person. I just tear myself apart. \n\nAnd I'll read back over it the next day and it just makes me feel even worse, but in the moment, I just can't stop the self-criticism. It's like I have to punish myself for what I just did. I'll sit up for hours just berating myself in my head and on paper, completely ruining whatever sleep I was supposed to get before my early shift. So, yeah... the guilt is always there, every single time, and it's honestly the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely drains whatever little bit of self-esteem I have left after a long week of scrubbing floors.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have these eating episodes more than once a week, or is it always just once?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 24 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"For the past several months, there has usually been at least one night each week where I buy too much food after work and eat it all in one stretch. I can’t remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's usually just the one night a week, honestly. But it's been happening like that for months now. I mean, I try to track it sometimes, in that same journal where I write all those awful things about myself, just to see if there's a pattern, you know? Like, is it always on a Thursday? Is it always after I have to clean the third-floor bathrooms, which are always the absolute worst? But it really just seems to be at least once a week, pretty consistently. 

I actually can't remember the last time I went a full month without it happening. Like, I don't think there's been a single month recently where I've managed to stop for more than a week, maybe a week and a half if I'm really, really lucky and the supervisor hasn't been breathing down my neck. But inevitably, that one night rolls around, usually when I'm just so bone-tired from my shift, and my feet are aching in those terrible work shoes, and I'll stop at the convenience store on the corner by my apartment. The one with the really bright fluorescent lights that always make me squint. And I'll just buy way too much food—the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I'll take it all home and eat it all in one sitting, just like I described before. 

So yeah, it's almost always just that one night a week, but it's like clockwork. It's just this constant, looming thing, and even when I have a good few days where I'm eating normally, packing a sensible lunch for work, maybe doing a little sketching in the evening... I always know that one night is coming. It just hasn't stopped for any real length of time in several months.

Cleaning offices and scrubbing floors, you're constantly moving, bending, lifting heavy trash bags, pushing those big industrial carts around. By the time I get home, my legs feel like lead, and my lower back is just aching. The last thing I want to do is go for a run or hit a gym. I mean, I used to try doing those little home workout videos back when I was taking college classes, just to relieve stress, but now I just don't have the energy. \n\nSo, yeah, after an episode, I just kind of sit there. I don't try to burn it off or get rid of it. I just sit there with my journal, feeling miserable and full, and let the guilt wash over me. I just take it, I guess. I don't try to undo it, because I know I did it to myself, so I just sit with the physical discomfort and the mental disgust until I eventually drag myself to bed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I mean, for the past several months, it feels like it's just become this awful routine. Usually, there's at least one night each week where it happens. It's almost always after work. I'll finish my shift, and I'm just so tired from being on my feet, sweeping and mopping and dealing with the industrial cleaners all day, and I'll stop by the grocery store on my way home. I always tell myself I'm just going in to get a normal dinner, maybe some chicken or a salad, but then I just start grabbing things. Cookies, chips, a whole pizza, ice cream—just way too much food. \n\nAnd then I get back to my apartment, and I just eat it all in one stretch. Just sit there on my couch, maybe put on a show I don't even really watch, and I just consume everything I bought until it's gone. And honestly, I can't even remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Like, maybe there was one week where I managed to just come home and eat a normal meal and go to sleep, but then the very next week, I'm right back at the store, filling up a basket with junk food. It's just this constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I tell myself that *this* week is going to be different, that *this* week I'm going to be better. But it just keeps happening, week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes and the feelings that follow significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.\n\nBut... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. So, yeah, I guess it doesn't stop me from functioning, like, I still pay my rent and show up where I need to be, but it definitely casts this huge, dark shadow over everything else I try to do.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes happen more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was kind of mentioning earlier, it's usually just the one time. I mean, for the past several months, it's been pretty consistently at least one night each week where I do that whole routine—you know, stopping after work, buying way too much food, and then just sitting there on my couch and eating it all in one stretch. \n\nOccasionally it might happen a second time if it's been a really, really bad week at the building—like if someone spilled something awful in the lobby or if I'm just feeling extra down about not being in my design classes—but generally, it's just that one night a week. And I've actually tried to think back, like, looking at the calendar app on my phone to see if there was a time I managed to break the cycle, but honestly, I can't remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. \n\nIt's like I can never quite make it to that two-week mark. I'll get through five or six days and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, Fumi, you're doing so well, you're just eating normal dinners,' and then bam, I'm right back at the store with my arms full of ramen and sweets. It's just so frustrating because it's like clockwork. You'd think if it's mostly just once a week, I could just power through that one evening and stop myself, but I just can't seem to do it. It just keeps happening, week after week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating or the pace at which you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like I completely lose the steering wheel, you know? Like, I'll be sitting there on my bed, still in my uniform, and I've just finished the ramen, and before I even really register what I'm doing, my hand is already unwrapping one of those tuna mayo rice balls. And it's not like I'm hungry anymore, not really, but I just keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. \n\nIt's this really weird disconnect. Because in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop it, you don't need this, you have sketches to finish, you wanted to practice that shading technique tonight.' But my body just isn't listening to my brain. I just keep chewing and swallowing and reaching for the fried chicken, and then the gummy candies. I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, every last wrapper empty, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't force another bite down. It's like a runaway train, and I'm just watching it crash from the inside. I hate it so much because I know I should be able to just put the food down, but in that moment, I just... I can't. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, it's funny you ask that because you'd think, with all the junk I'm eating on those nights, that I'd be putting on a ton of weight, right? But honestly, my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did when I got hired six months ago. They're these terrible, stiff navy blue trousers, and they have this really unforgiving waistband, so I feel like I would definitely notice if I was gaining weight because they'd start digging into my stomach when I'm bending over to mop or whatever. \n\nBut no, my weight has been pretty stable. I don't own a scale—I used to, back when I was still in school and living in the dorms, but when I moved into my current apartment after I dropped out, I just never bothered to buy a new one. But I can just tell by how my clothes fit. Like, even my favorite pair of jeans, the ones I wear when I go to those design events, they still zip up just fine. \n\nI guess maybe it's because my job is so physical? I'm on my feet for eight hours a day, walking up and down hallways, pushing that heavy cart, scrubbing things. It's a lot of manual labor, so maybe I'm just burning off all those extra calories from the ramen and the fried chicken? Or maybe because I don't eat much the day after a binge, because I feel so sick and guilty, it kind of balances out? I don't know. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. It's just the same as it's been for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself after these eating episodes every time they happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Every single time. It's actually... well, it's the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because the physical part is bad, feeling so full and sick, but the mental part afterwards is just... it's exhausting. \n\nThe minute I finish that last bite, or when I finally just physically can't eat anymore, this wave of just absolute disgust washes over me. I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. And it's not just feeling physically gross, it's this really intense disappointment in myself. I just sit there thinking, 'Fumi, you are twenty-four years old, you should be able to control yourself. You should have more willpower than a child in a candy store.' \n\nAnd then I usually end up grabbing my journal—the one I'm supposed to be using for my design ideas and sketching, you know, to keep my creative skills sharp—and instead of drawing, I just start writing these really awful, hateful things about myself. Pages and pages of it. I'll write about how weak I am, how I'm a failure, how I couldn't even finish college and now I can't even eat dinner like a normal person. I just tear myself apart. \n\nAnd I'll read back over it the next day and it just makes me feel even worse, but in the moment, I just can't stop the self-criticism. It's like I have to punish myself for what I just did. I'll sit up for hours just berating myself in my head and on paper, completely ruining whatever sleep I was supposed to get before my early shift. So, yeah... the guilt is always there, every single time, and it's honestly the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely drains whatever little bit of self-esteem I have left after a long week of scrubbing floors.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these eating episodes more than once a week, or is it always just once?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's usually just the one night a week, honestly. But it's been happening like that for months now. I mean, I try to track it sometimes, in that same journal where I write all those awful things about myself, just to see if there's a pattern, you know? Like, is it always on a Thursday? Is it always after I have to clean the third-floor bathrooms, which are always the absolute worst? But it really just seems to be at least once a week, pretty consistently. \n\nI actually can't remember the last time I went a full month without it happening. Like, I don't think there's been a single month recently where I've managed to stop for more than a week, maybe a week and a half if I'm really, really lucky and the supervisor hasn't been breathing down my neck. But inevitably, that one night rolls around, usually when I'm just so bone-tired from my shift, and my feet are aching in those terrible work shoes, and I'll stop at the convenience store on the corner by my apartment. The one with the really bright fluorescent lights that always make me squint. And I'll just buy way too much food—the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I'll take it all home and eat it all in one sitting, just like I described before. \n\nSo yeah, it's almost always just that one night a week, but it's like clockwork. It's just this constant, looming thing, and even when I have a good few days where I'm eating normally, packing a sensible lunch for work, maybe doing a little sketching in the evening... I always know that one night is coming. It just hasn't stopped for any real length of time in several months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I definitely do. It's... it's actually kind of frantic, the way I eat when it happens. Like, normally, I'm a pretty slow eater. If I'm just having a sandwich on my lunch break in the utility closet—because the break room is always so loud and I just want some quiet—I'll take my time, you know? I'll chew slowly, maybe read a little bit on my phone. 

But during these nights? It's completely different. I eat really, really fast. It's almost like I'm in a race against myself, or like I'm afraid someone is going to come in and take the food away, even though I live alone and my apartment door is locked. I don't even bother to sit down at my little kitchen table half the time. I'll just stand right there at the counter, still in my work uniform, not even taking my coat off sometimes, and I'll just start tearing into the packaging. Or if I do make it to my bedroom, I'll be sitting on my bed with my sketchbook still open from earlier, and I'll just be eating so quickly that I'm barely even tasting anything. 

It's not about enjoying the food at all. It's just about getting it down as fast as physically possible. It's like my brain goes on autopilot and my hands are just moving food to my mouth without me even really processing it. I think... I think maybe I do it so fast because if I slow down, I might actually think about what I'm doing. If I pause for even a second between the ramen and the rice balls, my brain might catch up and say, 'Fumi, what are you doing? Stop.' So I just keep going, faster and faster, just trying to get it all down before the guilt can kick in, before I can think too much about it. It's like I'm trying to outrun my own thoughts by eating, if that makes any sense. And I'll just keep that frantic pace up until everything is gone or until my stomach physically hurts too much to swallow another bite.

It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. It's like a runaway train, and I'm just watching it crash from the inside. I hate it so much because I know I should be able to just put the food down, but in that moment, I just... I can't. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. But honestly, my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did when I got hired six months ago. They're these terrible, stiff navy blue trousers, and they have this really unforgiving waistband, so I feel like I would definitely notice if I was gaining weight because they'd start digging into my stomach when I'm bending over to mop or whatever. \n\nBut no, my weight has been pretty stable. I don't own a scale—I used to, back when I was still in school and living in the dorms, but when I moved into my current apartment after I dropped out, I just never bothered to buy a new one. But I can just tell by how my clothes fit. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. It's just the same as it's been for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself after these eating episodes every time they happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Every single time. I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. So, yeah... the guilt is always there, every single time, and it's honestly the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely drains whatever little bit of self-esteem I have left after a long week of scrubbing floors.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these eating episodes more than once a week, or is it always just once?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's usually just the one night a week, honestly. But it's been happening like that for months now. But inevitably, that one night rolls around, usually when I'm just so bone-tired from my shift, and my feet are aching in those terrible work shoes, and I'll stop at the convenience store on the corner by my apartment. The one with the really bright fluorescent lights that always make me squint. And I'll just buy way too much food—the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I'll take it all home and eat it all in one sitting, just like I described before. \n\nSo yeah, it's almost always just that one night a week, but it's like clockwork. It's just this constant, looming thing, and even when I have a good few days where I'm eating normally, packing a sensible lunch for work, maybe doing a little sketching in the evening... I always know that one night is coming. It just hasn't stopped for any real length of time in several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. But it just keeps happening, week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes and the feelings that follow significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.\n\nBut... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. So, yeah, I guess it doesn't stop me from functioning, like, I still pay my rent and show up where I need to be, but it definitely casts this huge, dark shadow over everything else I try to do.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes happen more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was kind of mentioning earlier, it's usually just the one time. I mean, for the past several months, it's been pretty consistently at least one night each week where I do that whole routine—you know, stopping after work, buying way too much food, and then just sitting there on my couch and eating it all in one stretch. \n\nOccasionally it might happen a second time if it's been a really, really bad week at the building—like if someone spilled something awful in the lobby or if I'm just feeling extra down about not being in my design classes—but generally, it's just that one night a week. And I've actually tried to think back, like, looking at the calendar app on my phone to see if there was a time I managed to break the cycle, but honestly, I can't remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. \n\nIt's like I can never quite make it to that two-week mark. I'll get through five or six days and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, Fumi, you're doing so well, you're just eating normal dinners,' and then bam, I'm right back at the store with my arms full of ramen and sweets. It's just so frustrating because it's like clockwork. You'd think if it's mostly just once a week, I could just power through that one evening and stop myself, but I just can't seem to do it. It just keeps happening, week after week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating or the pace at which you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like I completely lose the steering wheel, you know? Like, I'll be sitting there on my bed, still in my uniform, and I've just finished the ramen, and before I even really register what I'm doing, my hand is already unwrapping one of those tuna mayo rice balls. And it's not like I'm hungry anymore, not really, but I just keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. \n\nIt's this really weird disconnect. Because in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop it, you don't need this, you have sketches to finish, you wanted to practice that shading technique tonight.' But my body just isn't listening to my brain. I just keep chewing and swallowing and reaching for the fried chicken, and then the gummy candies. I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, every last wrapper empty, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't force another bite down. It's like a runaway train, and I'm just watching it crash from the inside. I hate it so much because I know I should be able to just put the food down, but in that moment, I just... I can't. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, it's funny you ask that because you'd think, with all the junk I'm eating on those nights, that I'd be putting on a ton of weight, right? But honestly, my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did when I got hired six months ago. They're these terrible, stiff navy blue trousers, and they have this really unforgiving waistband, so I feel like I would definitely notice if I was gaining weight because they'd start digging into my stomach when I'm bending over to mop or whatever. \n\nBut no, my weight has been pretty stable. I don't own a scale—I used to, back when I was still in school and living in the dorms, but when I moved into my current apartment after I dropped out, I just never bothered to buy a new one. But I can just tell by how my clothes fit. Like, even my favorite pair of jeans, the ones I wear when I go to those design events, they still zip up just fine. \n\nI guess maybe it's because my job is so physical? I'm on my feet for eight hours a day, walking up and down hallways, pushing that heavy cart, scrubbing things. It's a lot of manual labor, so maybe I'm just burning off all those extra calories from the ramen and the fried chicken? Or maybe because I don't eat much the day after a binge, because I feel so sick and guilty, it kind of balances out? I don't know. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. It's just the same as it's been for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself after these eating episodes every time they happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Every single time. It's actually... well, it's the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because the physical part is bad, feeling so full and sick, but the mental part afterwards is just... it's exhausting. \n\nThe minute I finish that last bite, or when I finally just physically can't eat anymore, this wave of just absolute disgust washes over me. I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. And it's not just feeling physically gross, it's this really intense disappointment in myself. I just sit there thinking, 'Fumi, you are twenty-four years old, you should be able to control yourself. You should have more willpower than a child in a candy store.' \n\nAnd then I usually end up grabbing my journal—the one I'm supposed to be using for my design ideas and sketching, you know, to keep my creative skills sharp—and instead of drawing, I just start writing these really awful, hateful things about myself. Pages and pages of it. I'll write about how weak I am, how I'm a failure, how I couldn't even finish college and now I can't even eat dinner like a normal person. I just tear myself apart. \n\nAnd I'll read back over it the next day and it just makes me feel even worse, but in the moment, I just can't stop the self-criticism. It's like I have to punish myself for what I just did. I'll sit up for hours just berating myself in my head and on paper, completely ruining whatever sleep I was supposed to get before my early shift. So, yeah... the guilt is always there, every single time, and it's honestly the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely drains whatever little bit of self-esteem I have left after a long week of scrubbing floors.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these eating episodes more than once a week, or is it always just once?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's usually just the one night a week, honestly. But it's been happening like that for months now. I mean, I try to track it sometimes, in that same journal where I write all those awful things about myself, just to see if there's a pattern, you know? Like, is it always on a Thursday? Is it always after I have to clean the third-floor bathrooms, which are always the absolute worst? But it really just seems to be at least once a week, pretty consistently. \n\nI actually can't remember the last time I went a full month without it happening. Like, I don't think there's been a single month recently where I've managed to stop for more than a week, maybe a week and a half if I'm really, really lucky and the supervisor hasn't been breathing down my neck. But inevitably, that one night rolls around, usually when I'm just so bone-tired from my shift, and my feet are aching in those terrible work shoes, and I'll stop at the convenience store on the corner by my apartment. The one with the really bright fluorescent lights that always make me squint. And I'll just buy way too much food—the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I'll take it all home and eat it all in one sitting, just like I described before. \n\nSo yeah, it's almost always just that one night a week, but it's like clockwork. It's just this constant, looming thing, and even when I have a good few days where I'm eating normally, packing a sensible lunch for work, maybe doing a little sketching in the evening... I always know that one night is coming. It just hasn't stopped for any real length of time in several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It's... it's actually kind of frantic, the way I eat when it happens. Like, normally, I'm a pretty slow eater. If I'm just having a sandwich on my lunch break in the utility closet—because the break room is always so loud and I just want some quiet—I'll take my time, you know? I'll chew slowly, maybe read a little bit on my phone. \n\nBut during these nights? It's completely different. I eat really, really fast. It's almost like I'm in a race against myself, or like I'm afraid someone is going to come in and take the food away, even though I live alone and my apartment door is locked. I don't even bother to sit down at my little kitchen table half the time. I'll just stand right there at the counter, still in my work uniform, not even taking my coat off sometimes, and I'll just start tearing into the packaging. Or if I do make it to my bedroom, I'll be sitting on my bed with my sketchbook still open from earlier, and I'll just be eating so quickly that I'm barely even tasting anything. \n\nIt's not about enjoying the food at all. It's just about getting it down as fast as physically possible. It's like my brain goes on autopilot and my hands are just moving food to my mouth without me even really processing it. I think... I think maybe I do it so fast because if I slow down, I might actually think about what I'm doing. If I pause for even a second between the ramen and the rice balls, my brain might catch up and say, 'Fumi, what are you doing? Stop.' So I just keep going, faster and faster, just trying to get it all down before the guilt can kick in, before I can think too much about it. It's like I'm trying to outrun my own thoughts by eating, if that makes any sense. And I'll just keep that frantic pace up until everything is gone or until my stomach physically hurts too much to swallow another bite.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

And I'll just keep that frantic pace up until everything is gone or until my stomach physically hurts too much to swallow another bite.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes and that it's causing you significant distress?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 24 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Control\nmanifestation: \"Once I start, it feels like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, and even while I’m thinking, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish,' I don’t actually stop until the food is gone or I feel too uncomfortable.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. That's... that's exactly it. It's the lack of control that really gets to me, and it is so, so distressing. Because it's not like I'm just sitting there happily eating and losing track of time. It's like... well, once I start, it feels like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. Like my hands just have a mind of their own. 

I'll be sitting there on my bed, and my sketchbook will be right there next to me, maybe open to a drawing I started on my lunch break in the utility closet, and my brain is literally yelling at me. I'm sitting there thinking, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish. You wanted to work on that shading tonight.' I'm fully aware that I'm doing it, and I'm actively telling myself to quit, but my hand is just reaching into the bag for the next thing anyway. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body, just watching myself do this. 

And I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, or until my stomach is just so stretched and I feel too uncomfortable to physically force another bite down. It makes me feel so helpless. Like, I'm twenty-four, I work hard scrubbing those floors, I pay my own rent, but I can't even tell my own hands to stop opening a candy wrapper. It's just incredibly upsetting to feel that disconnected from your own actions, you know? It just makes me feel like I have absolutely no say in what I'm doing once that switch flips.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, um, thank you. Well, I guess I'm here because... I've been having these, um, these eating episodes, I guess you could call them. I mean, I know some people do, I've read about it, but I just... I don't. I don't throw up. The thought of throwing up actually really scares me, to be honest. When I was younger, I got food poisoning once from this little diner near my apartment—it was awful, I was sick for three days straight, couldn't even keep water down—and ever since then, I just can't stand the feeling of nausea or vomiting. So, no, I definitely don't do that. \n\nAnd as for exercising... well, I'm on my feet all day for work. Cleaning offices and scrubbing floors, you're constantly moving, bending, lifting heavy trash bags, pushing those big industrial carts around. By the time I get home, my legs feel like lead, and my lower back is just aching. The last thing I want to do is go for a run or hit a gym. I mean, I used to try doing those little home workout videos back when I was taking college classes, just to relieve stress, but now I just don't have the energy. \n\nSo, yeah, after an episode, I just kind of sit there. I don't try to burn it off or get rid of it. I just sit there with my journal, feeling miserable and full, and let the guilt wash over me. I just take it, I guess. I don't try to undo it, because I know I did it to myself, so I just sit with the physical discomfort and the mental disgust until I eventually drag myself to bed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I mean, for the past several months, it feels like it's just become this awful routine. Usually, there's at least one night each week where it happens. It's almost always after work. It's just this constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I tell myself that *this* week is going to be different, that *this* week I'm going to be better. But it just keeps happening, week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes and the feelings that follow significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.\n\nBut... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. I mean, for the past several months, it's been pretty consistently at least one night each week where I do that whole routine—you know, stopping after work, buying way too much food, and then just sitting there on my couch and eating it all in one stretch. \n\nOccasionally it might happen a second time if it's been a really, really bad week at the building—like if someone spilled something awful in the lobby or if I'm just feeling extra down about not being in my design classes—but generally, it's just that one night a week. And I've actually tried to think back, like, looking at the calendar app on my phone to see if there was a time I managed to break the cycle, but honestly, I can't remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. \n\nIt's like I can never quite make it to that two-week mark. I'll get through five or six days and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, Fumi, you're doing so well, you're just eating normal dinners,' and then bam, I'm right back at the store with my arms full of ramen and sweets. It's like I completely lose the steering wheel, you know? I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, it's funny you ask that because you'd think, with all the junk I'm eating on those nights, that I'd be putting on a ton of weight, right? But honestly, my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did when I got hired six months ago. They're these terrible, stiff navy blue trousers, and they have this really unforgiving waistband, so I feel like I would definitely notice if I was gaining weight because they'd start digging into my stomach when I'm bending over to mop or whatever. \n\nBut no, my weight has been pretty stable. I don't own a scale—I used to, back when I was still in school and living in the dorms, but when I moved into my current apartment after I dropped out, I just never bothered to buy a new one. But I can just tell by how my clothes fit. Like, even my favorite pair of jeans, the ones I wear when I go to those design events, they still zip up just fine. \n\nI guess maybe it's because my job is so physical? I'm on my feet for eight hours a day, walking up and down hallways, pushing that heavy cart, scrubbing things. It's a lot of manual labor, so maybe I'm just burning off all those extra calories from the ramen and the fried chicken? Or maybe because I don't eat much the day after a binge, because I feel so sick and guilty, it kind of balances out? I don't know. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. It's just the same as it's been for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself after these eating episodes every time they happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Every single time. It's actually... well, it's the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because the physical part is bad, feeling so full and sick, but the mental part afterwards is just... it's exhausting. \n\nThe minute I finish that last bite, or when I finally just physically can't eat anymore, this wave of just absolute disgust washes over me. I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. And it's not just feeling physically gross, it's this really intense disappointment in myself. I just sit there thinking, 'Fumi, you are twenty-four years old, you should be able to control yourself. You should have more willpower than a child in a candy store.' \n\nAnd then I usually end up grabbing my journal—the one I'm supposed to be using for my design ideas and sketching, you know, to keep my creative skills sharp—and instead of drawing, I just start writing these really awful, hateful things about myself. Pages and pages of it. I'll write about how weak I am, how I'm a failure, how I couldn't even finish college and now I can't even eat dinner like a normal person. I just tear myself apart. \n\nAnd I'll read back over it the next day and it just makes me feel even worse, but in the moment, I just can't stop the self-criticism. It's like I have to punish myself for what I just did. I'll sit up for hours just berating myself in my head and on paper, completely ruining whatever sleep I was supposed to get before my early shift. So, yeah... the guilt is always there, every single time, and it's honestly the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely drains whatever little bit of self-esteem I have left after a long week of scrubbing floors.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these eating episodes more than once a week, or is it always just once?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's usually just the one night a week, honestly. But it's been happening like that for months now. I mean, I try to track it sometimes, in that same journal where I write all those awful things about myself, just to see if there's a pattern, you know? Like, is it always on a Thursday? Is it always after I have to clean the third-floor bathrooms, which are always the absolute worst? But it really just seems to be at least once a week, pretty consistently. \n\nI actually can't remember the last time I went a full month without it happening. Like, I don't think there's been a single month recently where I've managed to stop for more than a week, maybe a week and a half if I'm really, really lucky and the supervisor hasn't been breathing down my neck. But inevitably, that one night rolls around, usually when I'm just so bone-tired from my shift, and my feet are aching in those terrible work shoes, and I'll stop at the convenience store on the corner by my apartment. The one with the really bright fluorescent lights that always make me squint. And I'll just buy way too much food—the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I'll take it all home and eat it all in one sitting, just like I described before. \n\nSo yeah, it's almost always just that one night a week, but it's like clockwork. It's just this constant, looming thing, and even when I have a good few days where I'm eating normally, packing a sensible lunch for work, maybe doing a little sketching in the evening... I always know that one night is coming. It just hasn't stopped for any real length of time in several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It's... it's actually kind of frantic, the way I eat when it happens. Like, normally, I'm a pretty slow eater. If I'm just having a sandwich on my lunch break in the utility closet—because the break room is always so loud and I just want some quiet—I'll take my time, you know? I'll chew slowly, maybe read a little bit on my phone. \n\nBut during these nights? It's completely different. I eat really, really fast. It's almost like I'm in a race against myself, or like I'm afraid someone is going to come in and take the food away, even though I live alone and my apartment door is locked. I don't even bother to sit down at my little kitchen table half the time. I'll just stand right there at the counter, still in my work uniform, not even taking my coat off sometimes, and I'll just start tearing into the packaging. Or if I do make it to my bedroom, I'll be sitting on my bed with my sketchbook still open from earlier, and I'll just be eating so quickly that I'm barely even tasting anything. \n\nIt's not about enjoying the food at all. It's just about getting it down as fast as physically possible. It's like my brain goes on autopilot and my hands are just moving food to my mouth without me even really processing it. I think... I think maybe I do it so fast because if I slow down, I might actually think about what I'm doing. If I pause for even a second between the ramen and the rice balls, my brain might catch up and say, 'Fumi, what are you doing? Stop.' So I just keep going, faster and faster, just trying to get it all down before the guilt can kick in, before I can think too much about it. It's like I'm trying to outrun my own thoughts by eating, if that makes any sense. And I'll just keep that frantic pace up until everything is gone or until my stomach physically hurts too much to swallow another bite.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes and that it's causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's... that's exactly it. It's the lack of control that really gets to me, and it is so, so distressing. Because it's not like I'm just sitting there happily eating and losing track of time. I mean, for the past several months, it feels like it's just become this awful routine. Usually, there's at least one night each week where it happens. It's almost always after work. I'll finish my shift, and I'm just so tired from being on my feet, sweeping and mopping and dealing with the industrial cleaners all day, and I'll stop by the grocery store on my way home. I always tell myself I'm just going in to get a normal dinner, maybe some chicken or a salad, but then I just start grabbing things. Cookies, chips, a whole pizza, ice cream—just way too much food. \n\nAnd then I get back to my apartment, and I just eat it all in one stretch. Just sit there on my couch, maybe put on a show I don't even really watch, and I just consume everything I bought until it's gone. And honestly, I can't even remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Like, maybe there was one week where I managed to just come home and eat a normal meal and go to sleep, but then the very next week, I'm right back at the store, filling up a basket with junk food. It's just this constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I tell myself that *this* week is going to be different, that *this* week I'm going to be better. But it just keeps happening, week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a lot of food, and it all happens so fast. Like, on some nights after my cleaning shift—especially if it's been a really long one where I had to do the deep cleaning on the second floor, which always takes forever because the carpets there are a nightmare—I'll stop at the little convenience store right by my apartment building. I always walk in thinking, 'Okay, Fumi, just grab something quick and easy for dinner, you're tired, just get a normal meal.' \n\nBut then I get inside, and it's like I just lose my mind. I'll grab a big bowl of ramen, the really large ones, you know? And then I'll head over to the cold section and pick up several of those stuffed rice balls—tuna mayo, salmon, whatever they have left. Then I'll smell the hot food case and get a couple pieces of fried chicken, and then I'll swing by the candy aisle and grab a bunch of sweets, like chocolate bars or those little gummy candies I used to eat when I was studying for finals back in college. \n\nI'll carry this huge bag up to my room, and I swear, within an hour or two, it's all gone. I just sit on my bed, still in my work clothes smelling like bleach and floor wax, and I just eat all of it, one thing right after another. The ramen, the rice balls, the chicken, the sweets—just boom, boom, boom. It's an insane amount of food for one person to eat in that short of a time, especially when I only meant to grab a normal dinner. It all just vanishes so quickly, and then I'm just left sitting there with all the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes and the feelings that follow significantly interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit complicated, I guess. I mean, I still go to work. I haven't missed any of my cleaning shifts, even though sometimes I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish that just the thought of putting on my uniform makes me want to cry. But I drag myself out of bed, I take the bus, and I do my job. I scrub the floors, empty the trash, wipe down the counters—I do all of it. And I still go to these little design events I like to attend, too. I mean, I love design, it's what I went to college for before I had to drop out, so I try to stay connected to that world. I still show up to those things.\n\nBut... it does affect me. It really does. The day after one of these episodes, I'm just... I'm a mess, mentally. I feel so distracted at work. Like, I'll be mopping a hallway, and all I can think about is how much I ate the night before, how gross I feel, how much I hate myself for doing it again. The shame is just so heavy, you know? It's like this thick fog that I can't shake off. And it messes with my hobbies, too. I love to sketch, it's my favorite thing to do, but... well, I use the same journal for my sketches that I use to write down all my feelings after I binge. So, sometimes, I'll want to sketch, but I just can't bring myself to open the book, because I know if I do, I'll see those entries. I'll see all those awful, miserable things I wrote about myself when I was sitting there, stuffed and disgusted. So I just put it off. I don't sketch, I don't practice my design skills, I just avoid the journal entirely. So, yeah, I guess it doesn't stop me from functioning, like, I still pay my rent and show up where I need to be, but it definitely casts this huge, dark shadow over everything else I try to do.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes happen more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was kind of mentioning earlier, it's usually just the one time. I mean, for the past several months, it's been pretty consistently at least one night each week where I do that whole routine—you know, stopping after work, buying way too much food, and then just sitting there on my couch and eating it all in one stretch. \n\nOccasionally it might happen a second time if it's been a really, really bad week at the building—like if someone spilled something awful in the lobby or if I'm just feeling extra down about not being in my design classes—but generally, it's just that one night a week. And I've actually tried to think back, like, looking at the calendar app on my phone to see if there was a time I managed to break the cycle, but honestly, I can't remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. \n\nIt's like I can never quite make it to that two-week mark. I'll get through five or six days and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, Fumi, you're doing so well, you're just eating normal dinners,' and then bam, I'm right back at the store with my arms full of ramen and sweets. It's just so frustrating because it's like clockwork. You'd think if it's mostly just once a week, I could just power through that one evening and stop myself, but I just can't seem to do it. It just keeps happening, week after week after week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating or the pace at which you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what it feels like. It's like I completely lose the steering wheel, you know? Like, I'll be sitting there on my bed, still in my uniform, and I've just finished the ramen, and before I even really register what I'm doing, my hand is already unwrapping one of those tuna mayo rice balls. And it's not like I'm hungry anymore, not really, but I just keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. \n\nIt's this really weird disconnect. Because in my head, I'm literally yelling at myself. I'll be thinking, 'Fumi, stop it, you don't need this, you have sketches to finish, you wanted to practice that shading technique tonight.' But my body just isn't listening to my brain. I just keep chewing and swallowing and reaching for the fried chicken, and then the gummy candies. I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, every last wrapper empty, or until my stomach hurts so badly that I physically can't force another bite down. It's like a runaway train, and I'm just watching it crash from the inside. I hate it so much because I know I should be able to just put the food down, but in that moment, I just... I can't. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, it's funny you ask that because you'd think, with all the junk I'm eating on those nights, that I'd be putting on a ton of weight, right? But honestly, my uniform pants still fit exactly the same as they did when I got hired six months ago. They're these terrible, stiff navy blue trousers, and they have this really unforgiving waistband, so I feel like I would definitely notice if I was gaining weight because they'd start digging into my stomach when I'm bending over to mop or whatever. \n\nBut no, my weight has been pretty stable. I don't own a scale—I used to, back when I was still in school and living in the dorms, but when I moved into my current apartment after I dropped out, I just never bothered to buy a new one. But I can just tell by how my clothes fit. Like, even my favorite pair of jeans, the ones I wear when I go to those design events, they still zip up just fine. \n\nI guess maybe it's because my job is so physical? I'm on my feet for eight hours a day, walking up and down hallways, pushing that heavy cart, scrubbing things. It's a lot of manual labor, so maybe I'm just burning off all those extra calories from the ramen and the fried chicken? Or maybe because I don't eat much the day after a binge, because I feel so sick and guilty, it kind of balances out? I don't know. I've thought about it a lot, actually, because I always worry that one day I'm just going to wake up and none of my clothes will fit, and then I'll have to go ask my supervisor for a bigger uniform, which would be so incredibly humiliating. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. It's just the same as it's been for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself after these eating episodes every time they happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Every single time. It's actually... well, it's the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because the physical part is bad, feeling so full and sick, but the mental part afterwards is just... it's exhausting. \n\nThe minute I finish that last bite, or when I finally just physically can't eat anymore, this wave of just absolute disgust washes over me. I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. And it's not just feeling physically gross, it's this really intense disappointment in myself. I just sit there thinking, 'Fumi, you are twenty-four years old, you should be able to control yourself. You should have more willpower than a child in a candy store.' \n\nAnd then I usually end up grabbing my journal—the one I'm supposed to be using for my design ideas and sketching, you know, to keep my creative skills sharp—and instead of drawing, I just start writing these really awful, hateful things about myself. Pages and pages of it. I'll write about how weak I am, how I'm a failure, how I couldn't even finish college and now I can't even eat dinner like a normal person. I just tear myself apart. \n\nAnd I'll read back over it the next day and it just makes me feel even worse, but in the moment, I just can't stop the self-criticism. It's like I have to punish myself for what I just did. I'll sit up for hours just berating myself in my head and on paper, completely ruining whatever sleep I was supposed to get before my early shift. So, yeah... the guilt is always there, every single time, and it's honestly the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely drains whatever little bit of self-esteem I have left after a long week of scrubbing floors.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these eating episodes more than once a week, or is it always just once?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's usually just the one night a week, honestly. But it's been happening like that for months now. I mean, I try to track it sometimes, in that same journal where I write all those awful things about myself, just to see if there's a pattern, you know? Like, is it always on a Thursday? Is it always after I have to clean the third-floor bathrooms, which are always the absolute worst? But it really just seems to be at least once a week, pretty consistently. \n\nI actually can't remember the last time I went a full month without it happening. Like, I don't think there's been a single month recently where I've managed to stop for more than a week, maybe a week and a half if I'm really, really lucky and the supervisor hasn't been breathing down my neck. But inevitably, that one night rolls around, usually when I'm just so bone-tired from my shift, and my feet are aching in those terrible work shoes, and I'll stop at the convenience store on the corner by my apartment. The one with the really bright fluorescent lights that always make me squint. And I'll just buy way too much food—the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I'll take it all home and eat it all in one sitting, just like I described before. \n\nSo yeah, it's almost always just that one night a week, but it's like clockwork. It's just this constant, looming thing, and even when I have a good few days where I'm eating normally, packing a sensible lunch for work, maybe doing a little sketching in the evening... I always know that one night is coming. It just hasn't stopped for any real length of time in several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It's... it's actually kind of frantic, the way I eat when it happens. Like, normally, I'm a pretty slow eater. If I'm just having a sandwich on my lunch break in the utility closet—because the break room is always so loud and I just want some quiet—I'll take my time, you know? I'll chew slowly, maybe read a little bit on my phone. \n\nBut during these nights? It's completely different. I eat really, really fast. It's almost like I'm in a race against myself, or like I'm afraid someone is going to come in and take the food away, even though I live alone and my apartment door is locked. I don't even bother to sit down at my little kitchen table half the time. I'll just stand right there at the counter, still in my work uniform, not even taking my coat off sometimes, and I'll just start tearing into the packaging. Or if I do make it to my bedroom, I'll be sitting on my bed with my sketchbook still open from earlier, and I'll just be eating so quickly that I'm barely even tasting anything. \n\nIt's not about enjoying the food at all. It's just about getting it down as fast as physically possible. It's like my brain goes on autopilot and my hands are just moving food to my mouth without me even really processing it. I think... I think maybe I do it so fast because if I slow down, I might actually think about what I'm doing. If I pause for even a second between the ramen and the rice balls, my brain might catch up and say, 'Fumi, what are you doing? Stop.' So I just keep going, faster and faster, just trying to get it all down before the guilt can kick in, before I can think too much about it. It's like I'm trying to outrun my own thoughts by eating, if that makes any sense. And I'll just keep that frantic pace up until everything is gone or until my stomach physically hurts too much to swallow another bite.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes and that it's causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's... that's exactly it. It's the lack of control that really gets to me, and it is so, so distressing. Because it's not like I'm just sitting there happily eating and losing track of time. It's like... well, once I start, it feels like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. Like my hands just have a mind of their own. \n\nI'll be sitting there on my bed, and my sketchbook will be right there next to me, maybe open to a drawing I started on my lunch break in the utility closet, and my brain is literally yelling at me. I'm sitting there thinking, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish. You wanted to work on that shading tonight.' I'm fully aware that I'm doing it, and I'm actively telling myself to quit, but my hand is just reaching into the bag for the next thing anyway. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body, just watching myself do this. \n\nAnd I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, or until my stomach is just so stretched and I feel too uncomfortable to physically force another bite down. It makes me feel so helpless. Like, I'm twenty-four, I work hard scrubbing those floors, I pay my own rent, but I can't even tell my own hands to stop opening a candy wrapper. It's just incredibly upsetting to feel that disconnected from your own actions, you know? It just makes me feel like I have absolutely no say in what I'm doing once that switch flips.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, definitely more than three months. It's... it's actually been going on for about a year and two months now. I know the timeline pretty well because it all started right around the time I made this big decision to try and transition out of my cleaning job and into graphic design. 

I had taken some college classes a few years ago, you know, before the money ran out and I had to start working full-time just to afford my tiny apartment. But I've always loved drawing, always loved design. So, about fourteen months ago, I decided I was going to really go for it. I started taking on these small freelance design gigs, mostly just logos for local businesses or flyers for community events, things like that. But the thing is, I couldn't afford to quit my job at the building. I still had to pay rent, I still had to buy groceries, so I kept all my regular cleaning shifts. 

And that's when it all started. The stress of trying to juggle both... it was just too much. I'd be scrubbing toilets on the third floor all day, my back aching, just exhausted, and then I'd come home and realize I had a deadline for a logo design the next morning. The pressure just started building and building, and that's when those nights of stopping at the convenience store really began. It was like I needed an escape valve, and the food was the only thing I had energy for. So yeah, it's been a solid year and two months of this cycle, ever since I tried to take on both worlds at once. I just haven't been able to stop since.

I always know that one night is coming. I'll chew slowly, maybe read a little bit on my phone. \n\nBut during these nights? It's completely different. I eat really, really fast. It's almost like I'm in a race against myself, or like I'm afraid someone is going to come in and take the food away, even though I live alone and my apartment door is locked. I don't even bother to sit down at my little kitchen table half the time. I'll just stand right there at the counter, still in my work uniform, not even taking my coat off sometimes, and I'll just start tearing into the packaging. It's just about getting it down as fast as physically possible. It's like my brain goes on autopilot and my hands are just moving food to my mouth without me even really processing it. I think... I think maybe I do it so fast because if I slow down, I might actually think about what I'm doing. If I pause for even a second between the ramen and the rice balls, my brain might catch up and say, 'Fumi, what are you doing? Stop.' So I just keep going, faster and faster, just trying to get it all down before the guilt can kick in, before I can think too much about it. It's like I'm trying to outrun my own thoughts by eating, if that makes any sense.
