I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. And it's not just feeling physically gross, it's this really intense disappointment in myself. I just sit there thinking, 'Fumi, you are twenty-four years old, you should be able to control yourself. You should have more willpower than a child in a candy store.' \n\nAnd then I usually end up grabbing my journal—the one I'm supposed to be using for my design ideas and sketching, you know, to keep my creative skills sharp—and instead of drawing, I just start writing these really awful, hateful things about myself. Pages and pages of it. I'll write about how weak I am, how I'm a failure, how I couldn't even finish college and now I can't even eat dinner like a normal person. I just tear myself apart. \n\nAnd I'll read back over it the next day and it just makes me feel even worse, but in the moment, I just can't stop the self-criticism. It's like I have to punish myself for what I just did. I'll sit up for hours just berating myself in my head and on paper, completely ruining whatever sleep I was supposed to get before my early shift. So, yeah... the guilt is always there, every single time, and it's honestly the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely drains whatever little bit of self-esteem I have left after a long week of scrubbing floors.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these eating episodes more than once a week, or is it always just once?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's usually just the one night a week, honestly. But it's been happening like that for months now. I mean, I try to track it sometimes, in that same journal where I write all those awful things about myself, just to see if there's a pattern, you know? Like, is it always on a Thursday? Is it always after I have to clean the third-floor bathrooms, which are always the absolute worst? But it really just seems to be at least once a week, pretty consistently. \n\nI actually can't remember the last time I went a full month without it happening. Like, I don't think there's been a single month recently where I've managed to stop for more than a week, maybe a week and a half if I'm really, really lucky and the supervisor hasn't been breathing down my neck. But inevitably, that one night rolls around, usually when I'm just so bone-tired from my shift, and my feet are aching in those terrible work shoes, and I'll stop at the convenience store on the corner by my apartment. The one with the really bright fluorescent lights that always make me squint. And I'll just buy way too much food—the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I'll take it all home and eat it all in one sitting, just like I described before. \n\nSo yeah, it's almost always just that one night a week, but it's like clockwork. It's just this constant, looming thing, and even when I have a good few days where I'm eating normally, packing a sensible lunch for work, maybe doing a little sketching in the evening... I always know that one night is coming. It just hasn't stopped for any real length of time in several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It's... it's actually kind of frantic, the way I eat when it happens. Like, normally, I'm a pretty slow eater. If I'm just having a sandwich on my lunch break in the utility closet—because the break room is always so loud and I just want some quiet—I'll take my time, you know? I'll chew slowly, maybe read a little bit on my phone. \n\nBut during these nights? It's completely different. I eat really, really fast. It's almost like I'm in a race against myself, or like I'm afraid someone is going to come in and take the food away, even though I live alone and my apartment door is locked. I don't even bother to sit down at my little kitchen table half the time. I'll just stand right there at the counter, still in my work uniform, not even taking my coat off sometimes, and I'll just start tearing into the packaging. Or if I do make it to my bedroom, I'll be sitting on my bed with my sketchbook still open from earlier, and I'll just be eating so quickly that I'm barely even tasting anything. \n\nIt's not about enjoying the food at all. It's just about getting it down as fast as physically possible. It's like my brain goes on autopilot and my hands are just moving food to my mouth without me even really processing it. I think... I think maybe I do it so fast because if I slow down, I might actually think about what I'm doing. If I pause for even a second between the ramen and the rice balls, my brain might catch up and say, 'Fumi, what are you doing? Stop.' So I just keep going, faster and faster, just trying to get it all down before the guilt can kick in, before I can think too much about it. It's like I'm trying to outrun my own thoughts by eating, if that makes any sense. And I'll just keep that frantic pace up until everything is gone or until my stomach physically hurts too much to swallow another bite.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes and that it's causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's... that's exactly it. It's the lack of control that really gets to me, and it is so, so distressing. Because it's not like I'm just sitting there happily eating and losing track of time. It's like... well, once I start, it feels like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. Like my hands just have a mind of their own. \n\nI'll be sitting there on my bed, and my sketchbook will be right there next to me, maybe open to a drawing I started on my lunch break in the utility closet, and my brain is literally yelling at me. I'm sitting there thinking, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish. You wanted to work on that shading tonight.' I'm fully aware that I'm doing it, and I'm actively telling myself to quit, but my hand is just reaching into the bag for the next thing anyway. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body, just watching myself do this. \n\nAnd I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, or until my stomach is just so stretched and I feel too uncomfortable to physically force another bite down. It makes me feel so helpless. Like, I'm twenty-four, I work hard scrubbing those floors, I pay my own rent, but I can't even tell my own hands to stop opening a candy wrapper. It's just incredibly upsetting to feel that disconnected from your own actions, you know? It just makes me feel like I have absolutely no say in what I'm doing once that switch flips.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these eating episodes for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely more than three months. It's... it's actually been going on for about a year and two months now. I know the timeline pretty well because it all started right around the time I made this big decision to try and transition out of my cleaning job and into graphic design. \n\nI had taken some college classes a few years ago, you know, before the money ran out and I had to start working full-time just to afford my tiny apartment. I started taking on these small freelance design gigs, mostly just logos for local businesses or flyers for community events, things like that. But the thing is, I couldn't afford to quit my job at the building. I still had to pay rent, I still had to buy groceries, so I kept all my regular cleaning shifts. \n\nAnd that's when it all started. The stress of trying to juggle both... it was just too much. I'd be scrubbing toilets on the third floor all day, my back aching, just exhausted, and then I'd come home and realize I had a deadline for a logo design the next morning. The pressure just started building and building, and that's when those nights of stopping at the convenience store really began. It was like I needed an escape valve, and the food was the only thing I had energy for. So yeah, it's been a solid year and two months of this cycle, ever since I tried to take on both worlds at once. I just haven't been able to stop since.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically have these eating episodes at least once a week, every week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely at least once a week. Usually more, honestly, but never less than once a week. For the past several months, there has usually been at least one night each week where I just... I leave the building, I'm exhausted from being on my feet all day, my hands are dry from the cleaning chemicals, and I just walk straight to that convenience store on the corner of my street. I'll buy way too much food—like I said, the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I just eat it all in one stretch as soon as I get home. \n\nAnd it's consistent, too. Like, I can’t remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Even when I tell myself on a Sunday, 'Okay, Fumi, this week is going to be different. You're going to come home, you're going to make a healthy dinner, and you're going to work on your freelance designs,' by Wednesday or Thursday, the stress just piles up again. The client wants a revision, or my supervisor at the cleaning job is breathing down my neck about the streakiness on the lobby windows, and before I know it, it's Thursday night and I'm back at the store, loading up a basket, and then back in my room tearing through it all again. It's just a constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I promise myself I will.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no control over your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I know I kind of touched on this a few minutes ago, but yes, absolutely no control. None at all. It's exactly like I was describing before... once I start, it's like I just keep reaching for the next thing almost completely automatically. It's the strangest, most frustrating feeling in the world, because my logical brain is still turned on, you know? \n\nI'll be sitting right there on my bed, surrounded by wrappers, and I'm literally thinking to myself, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish. You have to get that shading done if you want to build your portfolio and finally get out of the cleaning job.' I'm having that whole rational conversation in my head, practically begging myself to just put the bag down and pick up my pencil. But my hand just keeps going back for the next thing, over and over, like it's completely disconnected from my brain. \n\nI just... I don't actually stop. I can't. I just keep chewing and swallowing, opening the next package, and I keep going until either all the food I bought from the corner store is completely gone, or until my stomach is just so stretched and I feel so incredibly uncomfortable that I physically cannot force another single bite down my throat. It’s just this total, complete disconnect between what I want to do and what my body is actually doing, and it drives me crazy because I'm usually such a disciplined person with my work and my art, but in those moments, I have zero say in the matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I eat so, so fast. It's actually kind of embarrassing how quickly I get through it all. Like, I don't even take the time to sit down at my little dining table or use a plate or anything civilized like that. Half the time, I'm just standing right there at the kitchen counter, still in my uniform smelling like industrial floor cleaner, just tearing into the bags. I'm just shoving the food in. I eat so fast that I'm barely even chewing, let alone actually tasting any of it. The ramen, the rice balls... it all just kind of blends into this one salty, carb-heavy blur. It's like I'm in this frantic rush to just get it all down, almost as if I'm afraid that if I slow down for even a second, my brain will catch up and I'll realize what I'm doing and how much I'm ruining my goals. So I just speed through it. I take these huge bites and swallow before I've even really broken the food down, just trying to get it into my stomach before I can think too much about the calories or the money I just wasted or the fact that I should be working on a logo design instead. It's just this desperate, high-speed inhaling of food until the bags are empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these eating episodes more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying earlier, it's definitely at least once a week. But so far, no, my weight hasn't really changed at all. It's just the same as it's been for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself after these eating episodes every time they happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Every single time. It's actually... well, it's the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because the physical part is bad, feeling so full and sick, but the mental part afterwards is just... it's exhausting. \n\nThe minute I finish that last bite, or when I finally just physically can't eat anymore, this wave of just absolute disgust washes over me. I look at all the empty wrappers and the plastic containers scattered across my bed, and I just feel so gross. And it's not just feeling physically gross, it's this really intense disappointment in myself. I just sit there thinking, 'Fumi, you are twenty-four years old, you should be able to control yourself. You should have more willpower than a child in a candy store.' \n\nAnd then I usually end up grabbing my journal—the one I'm supposed to be using for my design ideas and sketching, you know, to keep my creative skills sharp—and instead of drawing, I just start writing these really awful, hateful things about myself. Pages and pages of it. I'll write about how weak I am, how I'm a failure, how I couldn't even finish college and now I can't even eat dinner like a normal person. I just tear myself apart. \n\nAnd I'll read back over it the next day and it just makes me feel even worse, but in the moment, I just can't stop the self-criticism. It's like I have to punish myself for what I just did. I'll sit up for hours just berating myself in my head and on paper, completely ruining whatever sleep I was supposed to get before my early shift. So, yeah... the guilt is always there, every single time, and it's honestly the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely drains whatever little bit of self-esteem I have left after a long week of scrubbing floors.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these eating episodes more than once a week, or is it always just once?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's usually just the one night a week, honestly. But it's been happening like that for months now. I mean, I try to track it sometimes, in that same journal where I write all those awful things about myself, just to see if there's a pattern, you know? Like, is it always on a Thursday? Is it always after I have to clean the third-floor bathrooms, which are always the absolute worst? But it really just seems to be at least once a week, pretty consistently. \n\nI actually can't remember the last time I went a full month without it happening. Like, I don't think there's been a single month recently where I've managed to stop for more than a week, maybe a week and a half if I'm really, really lucky and the supervisor hasn't been breathing down my neck. But inevitably, that one night rolls around, usually when I'm just so bone-tired from my shift, and my feet are aching in those terrible work shoes, and I'll stop at the convenience store on the corner by my apartment. The one with the really bright fluorescent lights that always make me squint. And I'll just buy way too much food—the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I'll take it all home and eat it all in one sitting, just like I described before. \n\nSo yeah, it's almost always just that one night a week, but it's like clockwork. It's just this constant, looming thing, and even when I have a good few days where I'm eating normally, packing a sensible lunch for work, maybe doing a little sketching in the evening... I always know that one night is coming. It just hasn't stopped for any real length of time in several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It's... it's actually kind of frantic, the way I eat when it happens. Like, normally, I'm a pretty slow eater. If I'm just having a sandwich on my lunch break in the utility closet—because the break room is always so loud and I just want some quiet—I'll take my time, you know? I'll chew slowly, maybe read a little bit on my phone. \n\nBut during these nights? It's completely different. I eat really, really fast. It's almost like I'm in a race against myself, or like I'm afraid someone is going to come in and take the food away, even though I live alone and my apartment door is locked. I don't even bother to sit down at my little kitchen table half the time. I'll just stand right there at the counter, still in my work uniform, not even taking my coat off sometimes, and I'll just start tearing into the packaging. Or if I do make it to my bedroom, I'll be sitting on my bed with my sketchbook still open from earlier, and I'll just be eating so quickly that I'm barely even tasting anything. \n\nIt's not about enjoying the food at all. It's just about getting it down as fast as physically possible. It's like my brain goes on autopilot and my hands are just moving food to my mouth without me even really processing it. I think... I think maybe I do it so fast because if I slow down, I might actually think about what I'm doing. If I pause for even a second between the ramen and the rice balls, my brain might catch up and say, 'Fumi, what are you doing? Stop.' So I just keep going, faster and faster, just trying to get it all down before the guilt can kick in, before I can think too much about it. It's like I'm trying to outrun my own thoughts by eating, if that makes any sense. And I'll just keep that frantic pace up until everything is gone or until my stomach physically hurts too much to swallow another bite.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes and that it's causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's... that's exactly it. It's the lack of control that really gets to me, and it is so, so distressing. Because it's not like I'm just sitting there happily eating and losing track of time. It's like... well, once I start, it feels like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically. Like my hands just have a mind of their own. \n\nI'll be sitting there on my bed, and my sketchbook will be right there next to me, maybe open to a drawing I started on my lunch break in the utility closet, and my brain is literally yelling at me. I'm sitting there thinking, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish. You wanted to work on that shading tonight.' I'm fully aware that I'm doing it, and I'm actively telling myself to quit, but my hand is just reaching into the bag for the next thing anyway. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body, just watching myself do this. \n\nAnd I don't actually stop until the food is completely gone, or until my stomach is just so stretched and I feel too uncomfortable to physically force another bite down. It makes me feel so helpless. Like, I'm twenty-four, I work hard scrubbing those floors, I pay my own rent, but I can't even tell my own hands to stop opening a candy wrapper. It's just incredibly upsetting to feel that disconnected from your own actions, you know? It just makes me feel like I have absolutely no say in what I'm doing once that switch flips.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these eating episodes for more than three months?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely more than three months. It's... it's actually been going on for about a year and two months now. I know the timeline pretty well because it all started right around the time I made this big decision to try and transition out of my cleaning job and into graphic design. \n\nI had taken some college classes a few years ago, you know, before the money ran out and I had to start working full-time just to afford my tiny apartment. But I've always loved drawing, always loved design. So, about fourteen months ago, I decided I was going to really go for it. I started taking on these small freelance design gigs, mostly just logos for local businesses or flyers for community events, things like that. But the thing is, I couldn't afford to quit my job at the building. I still had to pay rent, I still had to buy groceries, so I kept all my regular cleaning shifts. \n\nAnd that's when it all started. The stress of trying to juggle both... it was just too much. I'd be scrubbing toilets on the third floor all day, my back aching, just exhausted, and then I'd come home and realize I had a deadline for a logo design the next morning. The pressure just started building and building, and that's when those nights of stopping at the convenience store really began. It was like I needed an escape valve, and the food was the only thing I had energy for. So yeah, it's been a solid year and two months of this cycle, ever since I tried to take on both worlds at once. I just haven't been able to stop since.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically have these eating episodes at least once a week, every week?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely at least once a week. Usually more, honestly, but never less than once a week. For the past several months, there has usually been at least one night each week where I just... I leave the building, I'm exhausted from being on my feet all day, my hands are dry from the cleaning chemicals, and I just walk straight to that convenience store on the corner of my street. I'll buy way too much food—like I said, the ramen, the rice balls, the candy—and I just eat it all in one stretch as soon as I get home. \n\nAnd it's consistent, too. Like, I can’t remember a recent month where it stopped for more than a week or so. Even when I tell myself on a Sunday, 'Okay, Fumi, this week is going to be different. You're going to come home, you're going to make a healthy dinner, and you're going to work on your freelance designs,' by Wednesday or Thursday, the stress just piles up again. The client wants a revision, or my supervisor at the cleaning job is breathing down my neck about the streakiness on the lobby windows, and before I know it, it's Thursday night and I'm back at the store, loading up a basket, and then back in my room tearing through it all again. It's just a constant, weekly cycle that I can't seem to break out of, no matter how much I promise myself I will.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no control over your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I know I kind of touched on this a few minutes ago, but yes, absolutely no control. None at all. It's exactly like I was describing before... once I start, it's like I just keep reaching for the next thing almost completely automatically. It's the strangest, most frustrating feeling in the world, because my logical brain is still turned on, you know? \n\nI'll be sitting right there on my bed, surrounded by wrappers, and I'm literally thinking to myself, 'Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish. You have to get that shading done if you want to build your portfolio and finally get out of the cleaning job.' I'm having that whole rational conversation in my head, practically begging myself to just put the bag down and pick up my pencil. But my hand just keeps going back for the next thing, over and over, like it's completely disconnected from my brain. \n\nI just... I don't actually stop. I can't. I just keep chewing and swallowing, opening the next package, and I keep going until either all the food I bought from the corner store is completely gone, or until my stomach is just so stretched and I feel so incredibly uncomfortable that I physically cannot force another single bite down my throat. It’s just this total, complete disconnect between what I want to do and what my body is actually doing, and it drives me crazy because I'm usually such a disciplined person with my work and my art, but in those moments, I have zero say in the matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I eat so, so fast. It's actually kind of embarrassing how quickly I get through it all. Like, I don't even take the time to sit down at my little dining table or use a plate or anything civilized like that. Half the time, I'm just standing right there at the kitchen counter, still in my uniform smelling like industrial floor cleaner, just tearing into the bags. Or, if I do make it to my room, I'm sitting on the edge of my bed with my sketchbook literally still open right next to me, like I'm trying to trick myself into thinking I'm going to start drawing at any second. \n\nBut I'm not drawing. I'm just shoving the food in. I eat so fast that I'm barely even chewing, let alone actually tasting any of it. The ramen, the rice balls... it all just kind of blends into this one salty, carb-heavy blur. It's like I'm in this frantic rush to just get it all down, almost as if I'm afraid that if I slow down for even a second, my brain will catch up and I'll realize what I'm doing and how much I'm ruining my goals. So I just speed through it. I take these huge bites and swallow before I've even really broken the food down, just trying to get it into my stomach before I can think too much about the calories or the money I just wasted or the fact that I should be working on a logo design instead. It's just this desperate, high-speed inhaling of food until the bags are empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these eating episodes more than once a week?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying earlier, it's definitely at least once a week. I mean, sometimes it might be twice if it's a really bad week, but for the past several months, it’s been a pretty solid guarantee that there is at least one night every single week where this happens. I get off my shift, I'm just completely drained from scrubbing floors and hauling trash bags that weigh half as much as I do, and I just go straight to that corner store. I buy way too much food, and I eat it all in one stretch. \n\nAnd it's just so persistent. Like, I genuinely cannot remember a recent month where I went, say, two weeks without doing this. It never stops for more than a week or so. I keep thinking, 'Okay, this is the week I break the cycle, this is the week I just come home and focus on my freelance portfolio,' but then Thursday rolls around, or Friday, and I'm right back there, doing the exact same thing. It's like clockwork, honestly. At least one night a week, every week, for months now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these eating episodes at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, I know I just kind of went on a whole tangent about that, but yes, definitely for the past three months. Honestly, it feels like it's been going on even longer than that in my head because it's just so exhausting, but if I really sit back and look at the timeline... yeah, for the last several months, without fail, it's been at least once a week. \n\nLike I was saying, it’s usually after a really grueling shift. You know, when my back is just aching from the mop bucket and my hands are completely dried out from the chemicals, and I just feel so drained that I can't even fathom sitting down to work on my art. That's when it hits. And it’s just this constant, weekly thing. I honestly cannot recall a single month recently where I was able to go, like, a full two weeks without having one of these episodes. It just doesn't happen. It might pause for a few days, maybe a week if I'm really, really trying to force myself to just come straight home and drink water or something, but it never stops for longer than that. It's just this relentless cycle, week in and week out, for months now."
